=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string s consisting of digits.
Return the number of substrings of s divisible by their non-zero last digit.
Note: A substring may contain leading zeros.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "12936"
Output: 11
Explanation:
Substrings "29", "129", "293" and "2936" are not divisible by their last digit. There are 15 substrings in total, so the answer is 15 - 4 = 11.

Example 2:

Input: s = "5701283"
Output: 18
Explanation:
Substrings "01", "12", "701", "012", "128", "5701", "7012", "0128", "57012", "70128", "570128", and "701283" are all divisible by their last digit. Additionally, all substrings that are just 1 non-zero digit are divisible by themselves. Since there are 6 such digits, the answer is 12 + 6 = 18.

Example 3:

Input: s = "1010101010"
Output: 25
Explanation:
Only substrings that end with digit '1' are divisible by their last digit. There are 25 such substrings.

 
Constraints:

1 <= s.length <= 10^5
s consists of digits only.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def countSubstrings(self, s: str) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's design code:

class Solution:
    def countSubstrings(self, s: str) -> int:
        # state[d] for d 1..9 maybe list length d
        states = [None] * 10
        for d in range(1,10):
            states[d] = [0]*d
        ans = 0
        for ch in s:
            c = ord(ch)-48
            new_states = [None]*10
            # For each d update
            for d in range(1,10):
                old = states[d]
                new = [0]*d
                # append c to all old substrings
                # Could optimize by precomputing mapping? Since c changes, mapping r -> (r*10+c)%d.
                # For each r:
                for r, val in enumerate(old):
                    if val:
                        new[(r*10 + c) % d] += val
                # single digit substring
                new[c % d] += 1
                new_states[d] = new
            states = new_states
            if c != 0:
                ans += states[c][0]
        return ans

Need verify states[c][0] after update counts substrings ending at current position divisible by c. Yes.

states = [[0]*d for d in range(10)]  # states[0] = []
ans=0
for ch in s:
    c = ord(ch)-48
    for d in range(1,10):
        old = states[d]
        new = [0]*d
        # local variables for speed
        # for r, val in enumerate(old):
        #    if val: new[(r*10+c)%d] += val

        new[c % d] += 1
        states[d] = new
    if c:

return ans

Let's maybe write final code with precomputed transitions for speed and clarity:

class Solution:
    def countSubstrings(self, s: str) -> int:
        trans = [
            [[(r * 10 + c) % d for r in range(d)] for c in range(10)]
            for d in range(10)
        ]
        states = [[0] * d for d in range(10)]
        ans = 0
        for ch in s:
            c = ord(ch) - 48
            for d in range(1, 10):
                old = states[d]
                new = [0] * d
                td = trans[d][c]
                for r, val in enumerate(old):
                    new[td[r]] += val
                new[c % d] += 1
                states[d] = new
            if c:
                ans += states[c][0]
        return ans

This is clean. But note trans[0]? We have d=0 in trans? `for d in range(10)` includes d=0, but inner `range(d)` for d=0 empty, c loop 10, okay. trans[0] is list of 10 empty lists. Not used. states[0] empty. Fine.

Could precompute `single_idx = [c % d for d in range(10)]`? Not needed.

Potential issue: In-place update with `states[d] = new` while `old = states[d]` references old list. After assignment, old still exists until next iteration. Good.

class Solution:
    def countSubstrings(self, s: str) -> int:
        states = [[0] * d for d in range(10)]
        ans = 0
        for ch in s:
            c = ord(ch) - 48
            for d in range(1, 10):
                old = states[d]
                new = [0] * d
                for r, val in enumerate(old):
                    new[(r * 10 + c) % d] += val
                new[c % d] += 1
                states[d] = new
            if c:
                ans += states[c][0]
        return ans

This is simpler. 4.5M modulo operations, fine. Let's estimate time: 4.5M inner iterations, each with modulo and list access. In Python, 4.5M is very small. Let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "for d in range(1, 10):" and "states[d] = new" while `old =
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def countSubstrings(self, s: str) -> int:
        states = [[0] * d for d in range(10)]
        ans = 0

        for ch in s:
            c = ord(ch) - 48

            for d in range(1, 10):
                old = states[d]
                new = [0] * d

                for r, val in enumerate(old):
                    new[(r * 10 + c) % d] += val

                new[c % d] += 1
                states[d] = new

            if c != 0:
                ans += states[c][0]

        return ans
```
